In the 1972 to 79 cohort, a spinal cord injury was a young man’s car crash.

The hook

The patient moved fifteen years to the right.

You might be thinking that number sounds high for this injury. Across nine year-of-injury cohorts, mean age at injury rose to 43.2, and “typical” in this essay always means that mean, not the median or the most common case. A newer data-sheet window, “since 2015” through August 2025, puts the current anchor at 44.3. That anchor is related, but it is not a tenth point in the older cohort series.

The clinical version: a rehab system built around the young trauma patient increasingly meets a middle-aged or older adult, often after a fall.

A sampled historyThe National SCI Database follows patients treated at SCI Model Systems centers. It is not a census of every U.S. injury.

1972 to 79 · 01

A young-trauma baseline.

Mean age 28.7. Vehicular crashes account for 46.9%; falls, 16.5%; sports, 14.4%.

1980 to 84 · 02

Age crosses 30.

Mean age 30.5. Violence rises to 16.0% while crashes remain the dominant cause.

1985 to 89 · 03

Falls pass one in five.

Falls 20.7%. The sports share falls to 10.1%; violence reaches 18.8%.

1990 to 94 · 04

The violence spike.

Violence peaks at 28.9%. It is the sharpest single-era break in the cause mix.

1995 to 99 · 05

The spike recedes.

Violence falls to 21.1%. Falls continue upward to 23.4%.

2000 to 04 · 06

Crashes rebound once.

Vehicular injuries return to 47.5%. The longer direction of travel still points down.

2005 to 09 · 07

The mean passes 40.

Mean age 40.5. Falls reach 27.7%; sports sit at 8.0%.

2010 to 14 · 08

The crossover approaches.

Falls cross 30%. The crash-fall gap narrows to 7.8 points.

2015 to 21 · 09

The last cohort point.

Mean age 43.2. Falls are 31.3%; vehicular injuries are 37.5%.

SINCE 2015 · 10

The current anchor.

Mean age 44.3. Falls reach 32.5%; crashes fall to 37.1%; sports are 7.6%; medical/surgical is 3.8%.

SURVIVAL LINES · 11

One line climbed. One stayed flat.

First-year survival improved since the 1970s. After the first year, the published overall life-expectancy estimates have not changed since the early 1980s; that overall flatness does not prove every subgroup gained nothing. The dashboard draws direction only; it invents no intermediate points.

Etiology crossover

Falls nearly caught car crashes.

Falls kept climbing while crashes kept sliding. Crashes still lead, but a 30.4-point gap narrowed to just 4.6 points.

You would expect the aging to show up everywhere, and it nearly does: Chen and colleagues3 found it across sex, race, and every cause except violence. People injured through violence stayed young.

14.4 → 7.6%Sports share fellA smaller share of all injuries, not a count of injuries; the diving-heavy early mix faded.
1.2 → 3.8%Medical/surgical share rosePart of the rise is bookkeeping: this category was separated from “Other” in later reporting, as noted below.
28.9%Violence peaked1990 to 94, nearly triple the 1970s share.
Correlation, not causeThe violence peak overlaps national firearm-violence trends, but this dataset alone cannot explain why it rose or fell.

Violence spike

One era broke the pattern.

Violence hit 28.9% of injuries in 1990 to 94, then fell back. These are cohort windows, not annual estimates; the inset does not invent values for 1988 to 1998.

The timing lines up with national gun-violence patterns, but that overlap is correlation only. This series can’t say why the spike rose or fell.

Where survival stalled

The first year improved. The years after it stalled.

Since the 1970s, first-year life expectancy for people with tSCI improved steadily. For those who survived that first year, the published overall life-expectancy estimates have not budged since the early 1980s, and they still sit below the general population. An unchanged overall average does not prove that every subgroup gained nothing; the source publishes no subgroup trend that would settle the question.

No decade-by-decade life-expectancy series exists in the NSCISC source; the directions below come from its historical summary, not plotted points.

Why the plateau mattersRespiratory disease and septicemia remain prominent causes of death long after the acute injury.

Life expectancy improved during the first year after injury since the 1970s; after the first year, the published overall estimates have been flat since the early 1980s, which does not rule out gains in subgroups the source does not break out. Figures here are period estimates, not individual prognoses.

Pneumonia and septicemia persist.

What rehab inherited

The patient changed while the stay compressed.

Roughly 18,482 new U.S. tSCI cases occur each year, excluding deaths at the scene. Within the Model Systems data through August 2025, 38,647 people were represented across 31 centers.

78.02%male among injuries since 2015
47.7%incomplete tetraplegia, the largest neurologic category
<1%complete neurologic recovery by discharge
44.3average age at injury since 2015

An older, often fall-injured population drags geriatric complexity into acute rehab: bone health, fall prevention, multimorbidity, respiratory reserve, skin integrity, and pressure-injury vigilance.

Current mixSince 2015: 55.7% non-Hispanic White, 24.4% non-Hispanic Black, 14.7% Hispanic, 2.8% Asian, 0.6% Native American, and 1.9% Other.
The compression of care

Fewer inpatient days, more complexity.

Only the published endpoints are shown. The display marks a definition break rather than drawing a false continuous series.

The new profile

Incomplete tetraplegia is now the most common category.

47.7%Incomplete tetraplegia
20.4%Incomplete paraplegia
19.6%Complete paraplegia
11.8%Complete tetraplegia
0.4%Normal at discharge

“Normal” here is the neurologic classification recorded at discharge, a category on the exam, not proof of full functional recovery.

The typical patient, by mean age, is now about 15 years older than the 1972 to 79 cohort. Rehab built for the young trauma patient is treating someone else.

Prevalence uncertaintyThe point estimate is 311,560 people; the source range is 261,168 to 399,079. Incidence and prevalence come from separate studies, not the Model Systems database.

Notes

Superscript numbers in the essay point here.

  1. NSCISC 2021 Annual Statistical Report, Table 36. Cohort percentages for 1972-79 through 2015-21. ↩
  2. NSCISC 2026 Data Sheet (through August 2025). Life-expectancy period estimates and "since 2015" anchors; awaiting expert cross-check. ↩
  3. Chen et al. Analysis of traumatic spinal cord injury by sex, race, and cause; the aging pattern held across groups except violence, which stayed young. ↩
  4. National incidence: Jain et al. 2015. National prevalence: Lasfargues et al. 1995. Separate estimates, not Model Systems counts. ↩
Methodology & sources

What the database can and cannot say

  • Model Systems sample: these era trends describe patients treated at federally funded SCI Model Systems centers, not a complete national registry. The NSCISC database page currently estimates capture at roughly 6% of new SCI cases in the United States. Scene deaths are excluded.
  • Separate national estimates: incidence comes from Jain et al. 2015; prevalence from Lasfargues et al. 1995. They are not counts from the Model Systems database.
  • Cohort definitions changed over time: 1972-79 through 2015-21 cohorts come from the 2021 Annual Statistical Report. "Since 2015" anchors use a still-growing window through August 2025 and are shown separately.
  • Complication categories changed: medical/surgical complications were separated from "Other" in later reporting.
  • Life expectancy: period estimates from the 2026 Data Sheet, awaiting expert cross-check.
  • Mean only: the dashboard plots each era's published mean age as a single dot on the age axis. The source table publishes no age distribution for these cohorts, so no histogram is drawn.
  • Chart data: era anchors and cause shares are in data/sci-cohort/.

Educational, not medical advice.

Essay 03 · Sports epidemiology

Is pickleball actually dangerous?

By a PM&R resident physician

Why this essay: Everyone suddenly plays pickleball, and the ER numbers looked scarier than they were.

Emergency visits nearly doubled. Admissions more than tripled. But the per-player line barely moved.
